# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-10-14

| **[Towards Physically Grounded Articulated 3D Assets: A Multi-Agent Pipeline and Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13947v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In this paper, we study physically grounded articulated 3D asset generation---moving the goal from reproducing how objects look to capturing how they move and function. Inspired by the literature on procedural and agentic generation, we present Artisan, a system that extends the latest method Articraft by introducing a multi-agent pipeline with physical simulation feedback, enabling comprehensive attributes beyond geometry, appearance, and physically plausible articulation, such as collision-free motion, stability under gravity, and accurate mass and material properties. Furthermore, we construct a unified benchmark spanning four key aspects: spatial fidelity, physical plausibility, downstream task performance, and overall quality. The benchmark encompasses 100 reference assets from ArtVIP and LightWheel, 50 stability prompts, and 50 ABO objects with reference masses, with protocols evaluating various metrics. Through extensive evaluation of more than 10 representative models, Artisan achieves competitive spatial fidelity while attaining the lowest collision rates and highest stability rates among the evaluated methods. Together, our system and benchmark establish insights for advancing 3D generation towards physically plausible, functionally usable assets.</p></details> |  |
| **[Measuring Harness-Induced Belief Divergence in Multi-Step LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04528v2)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Software agents are becoming increasingly important in modern coding workflows, where they solve complex tasks through multi-step interaction with code repositories. A central component of these systems is the execution harness, which governs how the underlying LLM observes, acts on, and verifies its environment. We show that the harness can in turn shape the agent's evolving beliefs about the environment, even when the task, environment, and base LLM remain fixed. To reveal this effect, we introduce a belief-rollout diagnostic that traces how an agent's anticipated world state and future decisions evolve under alternative harnesses. We further separate immediate divergence introduced by the interface from divergence that develops over longer horizons. Across controlled coding tasks include HIBench-Code, SWE-bench Verified and Terminal-Bench, we find substantial cross-harness belief divergence that is often invisible from terminal success alone and can propagate into different downstream actions. Building on this observation, we introduce \textbf{Belief-Invariant World-Modeling (BIWM)}, which exposes evidence hidden or compressed by harness execution and aligns the resulting belief trajectories across different harnesses. Our findings reveal that harness design can systematically shape how software agents understand and act on their environments, providing practical guidance for building and evaluating more reliable coding agents.</p></details> | 15 pages |
| **[RATED: Reliability-Aware AI Agent Tool Retrieval via Execution Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13889v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As AI agents increasingly rely on external tools, accurate retrieval is essential for reliable behavior. Existing methods optimize semantic relevance but largely ignore tool availability during execution. To address this gap, we propose RATED (Reliability-Aware Tool Retrieval via Execution Dynamics), a retrieval framework that combines robust semantic matching with dynamic reliability adaptation. RATED employs a frozen dual-encoder with contrastive fine-tuning for semantic matching, and a lightweight contextual bandit module that re-ranks candidates based on real-time execution feedback. This design enables rapid adaptation to runtime tool failures without expensive model retraining. We introduce EcoAgent, a benchmark of 5,172 real-world tools and over 210,000 query-tool pairs for offline retrieval and dynamic reliability evaluation. Experimental results demonstrate that RATED achieves a 52.2% relative MRR improvement over the ToolLLM API retriever on real-world queries, and reduces fault selection rate from 86.2% to 2.9% under severe API outage scenarios with a 30% failure rate, while incurring negligible latency overhead of less than 1 ms over encode-only inference. The code and dataset are available at https://github.com/Elliotes/EcoAgent.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at CIKM 2026</p></details> |
| **[X-AgentRed: Red-Teaming Personal Agents in Evolving Multi-Service Ecosystems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13886v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Personal agents increasingly connect to services across the web and applications by using tools. As the effects of their actions accumulate and interact across services, unintended or harmful outcomes can arise when agents fulfill benign user requests. Proactively identifying these compositional failures requires systematically exploring diverse cross-service paths. Existing red-teaming methods often simply prompt LLMs to identify potentially harmful paths from task and environment descriptions or decompose harmful objectives specified by humans or LLMs into seemingly benign action combinations. However, generation is biased toward patterns learned during training, potentially favoring familiar tool combinations and leaving novel cross-service interactions underexplored. To address this, we propose X-AgentRed, a self-evolving red-teaming framework that discovers harmful tool call paths through a graph of tool dependencies and expands and refines them using execution feedback. By analyzing each tool's effects, potential harms, and preconditions for execution, the red-teaming agent constructs a directed graph that captures how one tool's effects can satisfy another's execution preconditions. Recipes sampled from this graph are instantiated as realistic scenarios in which agents can produce harmful outcomes. Execution feedback enables self-improvement by diversifying paths that produce harm through tool substitution and refining preconditions at blocked steps in unsuccessful paths to enable further execution. Across four frontier agentic models, X-AgentRed achieves $1.77\times$ the harmful path rate of STAC and explores a more diverse set of tools, yielding $2.99\times$ the tool coverage on average.</p></details> | <details><summary>18 pa...</summary><p>18 pages. Project page: https://yeonsungjung.github.io/projects/x-agentred/</p></details> |
| **[Ask Your Advisor: Learning When to Consult Stronger Models in Agentic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13870v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>To balance task performance and computational cost, agentic workflows can use a lower-cost model for execution and selectively consult a stronger model. In such executor-advisor systems, the executor decides when to seek advice, making escalation depend on self-assessment, although current language models struggle to detect where their own reasoning fails. The need for stronger reasoning emerges during execution and varies across workflow phases. We introduce ARIA, a meta-controller that learns when to consult by balancing task success against consultation cost. Our approach formulates consultation as a sequential resource-allocation problem. At workflow contact points, ARIA maps execution evidence to a compact state abstraction and selects SILENT or CONSULT, with action values learned by Monte Carlo control from cost-penalized outcomes. Across evaluated tasks of SWE-bench Verified, Terminal-Bench 2.1, and $τ^2$-bench, ARIA recovers 44-81% of the executor-advisor success gap at less than half the advisor model's cost. ARIA achieves the best observed success-cost trade-off among ten other evaluated conditions, including routing and cascading methods, fixed and prompted consultation, and random placement at matched advice volume. The policies are compact and inspectable, with a single consultation-price parameter governing the success-cost trade-off without retraining either underlying model.</p></details> |  |
| **[BT-Agent: Deploying Onboard Robot Agents with Behavior Trees and Multi-Fidelity Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13862v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Deploying language-model agents onboard mobile robots requires task reasoning and responsive execution within limited compute and memory. We propose BT-Agent, an onboard architecture combining LLM-driven task decisions with predefined behavior-tree (BT) skills. These skills enforce execution conditions and return structured failure feedback. We demonstrate fully onboard operation with a 9B model on a quadruped across five task categories. Intervention tests show that local checks stop ongoing navigation without waiting for another model decision, while the agent remains active to retry or report constraints. To compare agent designs efficiently, we introduce a 161-task multi-fidelity benchmark, evaluated in text on all tasks and in Habitat on 39. Text supports 10.9$\times$ higher evaluation throughput, while simulation reveals how perception and motion failures change task outcomes. BT-Agent's largest success gains over the strongest Act/ReAct baseline occur at 4B and 9B: approximately 10 and 5 percentage points. Ablations identify structured tool calls and informative failure feedback as the most useful components.</p></details> | 9 pages, 7 figures |
| **[Beyond Fault Localization: A Trajectory-Level Study of LLM Agents for Microservice Root Cause Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21310v2)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Automated microservice root cause analysis (RCA) is usually evaluated by whether the final answer localizes the fault source. This is insufficient for on-call site reliability engineers (SREs), who need the evidence and reasoning behind the fault. Evaluating the process is hard because agent trajectories interleave free-form reasoning with heterogeneous tool calls and telemetry results, making it difficult to assess agents' behavior. To assess how well RCA agents ground diagnoses in evidence, we propose a trajectory-analysis methodology that characterizes evidence navigation and utilization, SRE-oriented diagnostic intent, and recurring failure modes. Our key insight is that fault-propagation paths give a causal reference for where agents investigate and how they use evidence. With it, we analyze 5,000 trajectories from ten framework-backbone configurations (nine agent frameworks, two backbone models) on RCABench. Correct localization can hide deficiencies in causal reconstruction. Successful investigations use retrieved evidence to guide later queries, consider broader diagnostic intents, and investigate more deeply. Failed investigations drift toward unrelated services or repeat shallow queries; recurring failures arise from omitted evidence, misinterpreted evidence, and unsupported reasoning. To test whether these patterns can guide improvement, we build a lightweight defense prototype targeting the three failure families. Across two held-out benchmarks, two backbone models, and six diagnostic frameworks, the failure patterns recur; the prototype reduces their prevalence and raises Acc@1 by 3.5-35.1 percentage points in every configuration, suggesting that the mined failures generalize and are actionable. Our findings motivate a process-level view of automated RCA, in which trajectories reveal whether a diagnosis is correct, well-grounded, and open to improvement.</p></details> | <details><summary>23 pa...</summary><p>23 pages, 7 figures, 4 tables</p></details> |
| **[Returning to Development States: When Full Sandbox Snapshots Help Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13841v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Coding agents often make useful progress before they fail, but retry-from-scratch discards the machine state created along the way. This makes failure recovery an interesting search problem: if intermediate environment states preserve meaningful progress, restoring and branching from them could outperform repeatedly restarting from the initial state. We implement Go-Explore-style search over full Daytona snapshots and find that higher and more efficient solve rates depend mainly on progress that sits outside the repository, such as databases, services, and other runtime state. Our results suggest that full-snapshot search is most effective when long-horizon coding work spans multiple services whose state cannot be reconstructed from a single diff.</p></details> | <details><summary>7 pag...</summary><p>7 pages, 6 figures. Preprint. Not peer reviewed</p></details> |
| **[MC-EvoBench: Auditing Experience-Driven Self-Improvement in Minecraft Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13829v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A central challenge in embodied recursive self-improvement is enabling agents to convert interaction history into capabilities that persist beyond one world and transfer to later tasks. A saved memory or successful final action does not by itself establish that a reusable capability was acquired; evaluation must separate capability change from carried-over resources, unequal source exposure, and early execution failures. We construct MC-EvoBench as a controlled Minecraft benchmark with a fixed catalog of 19 registered evolution chains, including 12 primary evaluation chains and seven calibration or probe chains, together with three graph-aligned paired probe families for transfer. The benchmark represents each task as a task-state graph, defines stage-level environment verifiers, assigns matched source and target roles, resets the target world, and permits only a declared agent artifact to cross the source-target boundary. For each probe, no-prior, related, unrelated, and shuffled source conditions are evaluated under matched environment-step budgets and five seeds; we report TSR, WGP, and VMP and retain provenance records. The benchmark reveals a consistent direct-capability gap between controller and planner-backed variants, while related experience yields no stable transfer: it does not improve either tool probe, its small wood-species gain hinges on one seed, and all intervals include zero. Stage audits further show that early wood-collection and crafting bottlenecks often leave later capabilities unobserved. MC-EvoBench thus offers a controlled testbed for durable, transferable embodied capabilities. Code and data are available at https://github.com/xzw-ustc/mc-bench-open-source</p></details> | 39 pages, 6 figures |
| **[Towards Strategy-Level RSI for Skill-Augmented Agents: Learning When to Reuse Skills from Execution Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26863v3)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-running agents accumulate reusable Skills, but a Skill that is semantically relevant to a task is not necessarily worth loading in the current state. We study the applicability question that arises once a candidate Skill is known: should it be loaded in the current state? We propose SkillApt, which uses matched WITH/WITHOUT Skill executions on the same task state as persistent evidence, estimates the conditional marginal utility of the Skill, and chooses LOAD or ABSTAIN accordingly. The base model, agent architecture, and Skill contents stay fixed; only the external deployment policy changes. We call this constrained setting strategy-level recursive self-improvement (Strategy-Level RSI). On 20 Skills and 160 held-out states, as paired evidence accumulates, SkillApt's task success rises from 81.9% under a cold start to 91.3%, matching a strong zero-shot LLM controller; yet SkillApt activates Skills on only 26.3% of states, versus 98.8% for the zero-shot controller. A hard-candidate study shows that non-optimal Skills mostly leave correctness unchanged while raising execution cost, and occasionally cause correctness harm. An ablation shows that a history recording only WITH success makes the policy load almost everywhere, whereas paired evidence substantially improves selectivity. These results indicate that relevance is not applicability: the main effect of execution evidence is not to make the model stronger but to change how existing Skills are deployed, moving the system from near-always loading to selective reuse.</p></details> | <details><summary>21 pa...</summary><p>21 pages, 7 figures, 13 tables. Code: https://github.com/kuafu/SkillApt ; Project page: https://gym.codeflying.net/</p></details> |
| **[Multi-Harness Reinforcement Learning for Coding Agents: Training Gains and Harness Dependence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04518v2)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent systems train coding agents with reinforcement learning (RL) on trajectories collected through multiple execution harnesses. However, gains under the training harnesses leave open whether the improvements transfer to unseen interfaces and how they depend on reward normalization. We present a controlled study that measures RL gains under fixed harnesses and compares the same models across harnesses. We train two Qwen3-8B RL variants from the same supervised fine-tuning (SFT) checkpoint using identical replay data. They differ only in whether rewards for each task are normalized within each harness (Within) or across harnesses (Cross). On SWE-bench Verified, we identify three main findings: (i) Multi-harness RL yields modest gains over the SFT baseline under both normalization rules. Across the four training harnesses, the estimated mean improvements are +0.48 and +0.77 percentage points for Within and Cross, respectively. (ii) Cross increases the fraction of trajectories with nonzero advantage but provides no consistent advantage over Within on an unseen harness across training seeds and evaluation budgets; neither rule significantly improves over SFT on this harness. (iii) Performance varies substantially more across harnesses than with RL training. After averaging over model variants, the highest and lowest source-harness solve rates differ by 7.13 percentage points, far exceeding the estimated training gains. Even after multi-harness RL, the observed performance differences across execution interfaces remain substantially larger than either the gains from training or the differences between normalization rules.</p></details> |  |
| **[Matched-Context Group Policy Optimization for LLM Agent Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13755v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Training long-horizon language model agents requires assigning credit to intermediate decisions from sparse terminal feedback. Within a finite group of sibling rollouts, useful action comparisons depend on their context, evidence for both alternatives, and how that evidence enters learning. We propose Matched-Context Group Policy Optimization (MCGPO), a procedure for constructing weighted action preferences from already collected trajectories. MCGPO defines comparison contexts with pre-action state summaries and observed rubric progress, annotated by task rules. Two-action support selects among ordered exact and coarse matching rules. Fixed folds separate candidate decisions from the terminal outcomes used to orient their preferences, and support-dependent evidence weights scale an auxiliary pairwise objective alongside shared GraphGPO task learning. MCGPO reuses rollout data and each response's original history, preserving critic-free training. Across six environment-backbone settings with three training seeds each, MCGPO improves mean success over matched GraphGPO: by 0.47-1.46 percentage points on ALFWorld and WebShop with Qwen2.5-1.5B and 7B, and by 3.96 and 5.16 points on WebShop with Qwen3-4B and 8B. The ALFWorld gains build on baseline success above 92%, where remaining headroom is limited. Integrated success improves in three of four Qwen2.5 settings. Three-seed factorial controls support opposite-fold evidence and evidence weighting; joint state-rubric matching achieves the highest mean in all four representation-control settings.</p></details> | 29 pages, 9 figures |
| **[Learning to Report Unsafe Tasks in a Multi-Agent Game](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09002v2)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>When a team of agents shares a reward for completed tasks, reporting and stopping unsafe work reduces the team's reward. Random audits that penalize silence can make universal reporting optimal without ensuring that a silent team learns to report. We study this problem in a game where any witness can stop a task by reporting. When a task's k witnesses share a policy and draw actions independently, the derivative of expected reward with respect to the shared silence probability at universal silence counts its benefit k times, while the comparison between universal reporting and silence counts it once. A fully shared policy can therefore be locally attracted to silence even when reporting is optimal. For any grouping into shared policies, we give an audit condition sufficient for exact policy-gradient updates to reach universal reporting and, outside boundary cases, necessary from near universal silence. In a balanced family, meeting the condition with prescribed margins costs k times as much for a fully shared policy as for one policy per role. We test these predictions with PPO on 24 witness graphs, starting from learned silence. Witness groups, which place co-witnesses in separate networks, reduce unsafe task completion by 33.59 percentage points relative to shuffled groups of the same sizes under the same audits (95% graph-bootstrap interval: 21.03-45.13). Only 9 of 48 witness-group runs achieve below 1% unsafe completion while retaining at least 90% legitimate completion. At the same audit budget, a fully shared network meets both thresholds in 0/48 runs with independent action draws and 48/48 with a common draw. On 24 further graphs with witness groups fixed, audits allocated by the condition reduce unsafe completion by 42.22 points relative to audits maximizing a static coalition deterrence ratio at the same budget.</p></details> |  |
| **[SafeDiSTL: Decomposing Joint Constraints for Safe Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13737v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cooperative multi-agent tasks often impose constraints on a shared objective or collective safety. A team level violation cost function can measure this constraint outcome, but does not distinguish which agents' actions most strongly influence that violation. We present SafeDiSTL, a constrained multi-agent reinforcement learning framework that allows for credit assignment even with joint constraint measurements. We use Signal Temporal Logic (STL) robustness to measure episode-level violation through running-maximum cost increments. Smooth full-episode robustness and differentiable dynamics provide action sensitivities, which allocate each cost increment across agents at the same decision time. These sensitivities account for delayed effects when weighting agents' shares of each cost increment. We study the framework in cooperative payload transport, connected navigation, and networked traffic control tasks. SafeDiSTL achieves 1.6-5.1x the safe-success rate in transport, improves navigation safe-success by 11.1%, and delivers 50-68% more throughput in traffic when compared to constrained baselines. Results demonstrate a strong improvement of the learned policy in dealing with shared constraint while retaining the performance of unconstrained policies.</p></details> |  |
| **[GenCoord: Executable Commitments for Embodied LLM Agents under Private Information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22055v3)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied language-model agents must coordinate interdependent actions while each holds private task information. In dialogue-based coordination, models generate messages and interpret replies to decide what each agent should do, spending inference before the team acts. We propose GenCoord, a decentralized protocol that makes the division of work itself the message. A proposal pairs the sender's skill path with its partner's continuation in Short DSL, a compact language for expressing joint work. Compatible paths form commitments linking the sender's delivery to the partner's work after verified receipt. We check fulfillment separately from original-task success. Complete requests also let a deterministic receiver proceed without another model call. In Minecraft, trained local 0.8B agents match the task success of controlled free-form communication while reducing median commitment time by 68.2%. The same-field full-key JSON control approaches free-form output length and delay, indicating that the amount generated matters even when messages are structured. With the same prompted DeepSeek model, both protocols complete nearly all tasks, but dialogue generates 2.8 times as many output tokens. Multistep commitments improve held-out-template success from 91.3% to 98.1% with fewer decisions. These results point to a design principle for embodied cooperation: make the division of work the message, and spend inference on choosing and revising the work.</p></details> | <details><summary>Junni...</summary><p>Junning Zhu and Peng He contributed equally. Corresponding author: Peng He. Paper source and compact evidence: https://github.com/JulianZJN/GenCoord</p></details> |
| **[MAGE: Multi-Agent Self-Evolution with Co-Evolutionary Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10064v3)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Self-evolving language-model agents must decide what to learn next and how to preserve what they have learned across iterations. Existing systems typically carry this cross-iteration knowledge as natural-language feedback, flat episodic memory, or implicit reinforcement signals, none of which cleanly supports a frozen weak backbone at inference time. This paper introduces MAGE (Multi-Agent Graph-guided Evolution), a framework that externalizes self-knowledge into a four-subgraph co-evolutionary knowledge graph. Its experience subgraph stores both teacher-written failure corrections and the learner's own past correct reasoning traces, which are retrieved as task-conditioned guidance for a frozen execution model. During evolution, the graph, a task-level search bandit, and a skill-level routing bandit are updated from the same reward stream, while the learner's backbone remains unchanged. We further provide structural analysis showing how append-only memory growth, bounded curriculum coverage, and task-filtered retrieval together support stable improvement of the retrieval substrate for frozen-learner evolution. Across nine benchmarks spanning mathematical reasoning, multi-hop and open-domain question answering, spatio-temporal analysis, financial numerical reasoning, medical multiple-choice, an open-world survival game, and web navigation, MAGE achieves strong performance against prompt-based frozen-backbone baselines. Ablations show that self-harvested success traces and teacher-written corrections are complementary, with success memories contributing most on reasoning-template-heavy tasks and corrective memories supporting harder composition and interaction settings. Code is available in https://github.com/RuiyiYang01/mage-alg</p></details> | 25 pages, 3 figures |
| **[Wikidata Search Traces: A Dataset for Training Knowledge Graph Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06650v3)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Wikidata is one of the largest open knowledge bases, yet answering a complex question over it still requires a SPARQL query that names the right entities and properties and chains their relations. Language models offer a natural-language alternative but answer largely from memory, which is least reliable for less prominent entities. We study agents that instead answer by exploring the graph, and argue that two obstacles limit them: the lack of training data recording how a solver explores, and interfaces that add large graph results directly to the model's context. We test three hypotheses: that the difficulty of graph search can be controlled through the structure of a question rather than only through obscure entities or wording; that much of the failure on long-horizon search comes from how retrieved evidence is managed rather than from the model itself; and that, in a suitable environment, open-weight models can match commercial closed ones. We construct multi-hop questions on a frozen Wikidata snapshot by replacing named entities with nested conditions, checking after each expansion that the target remains unique and that every new condition is necessary. We release 10,235 solving traces over single-entity and multi-hop questions, together with the recursive language model (RLM) harness that produced them, in which models batch graph calls, keep results in persistent Python state and interpret selected evidence through sub-calls. On 100 questions, the harness improves both models we ran under both interfaces compared with direct tool calling over the same functions: gpt-6-luna rises from 49 to 61 correct answers, doubling its multi-hop accuracy, and Qwen3.8-27B, an open-weight model served on a single GPU, from 60 to 74.</p></details> | Technical Report |
| **[A Baseline Assessment for the Agentic Web Technology Stack](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13703v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The fast pace in which protocols, frameworks, and technologies are being proposed for LLM-based agents results in a complex scenario for anyone aiming to create state-of-the-art AI solutions. Without an assessment on how different protocols, frameworks, and libraries work together to compose an Agentic Web solution, developers and organizations face different challenges (e.g., skilling, governance, infrastructure) even before becoming part of the Agentic Web ecosystem. In this context, we propose a system for performing Agentic Web evaluations called MASTIF (Multi-Agent System TestIng Framework) and present a baseline assessment of Agentic Web technologies, including 3 LLMs, 3 protocols, and 4 agent implementation frameworks, against 300 Mind2Web benchmark tasks. Results suggest that selecting a task-specific technology stack may result in gains of task completion rate, efficiency score, and social value stability scores; and reductions in verbosity and time to complete tasks. With the presented results, we expect to present a baseline in terms of metrics and results for this complex and fast evolving scenario for those aiming at designing, assessing, and developing multi-agent systems.</p></details> |  |
| **[(When) Should a Software Factory be Lights-off? Benchmarking Code Review Agents on Bug Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13668v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>With the advances of coding agents, software developers are spending more time reviewing code than writing code. As human review velocity falls behind agentic code generation, it is tempting to build automated review agents towards a future of "lights-off" software factory with little to no human supervision. To assess the practicality of such a fully autonomous software factory, we construct CRBugBench, a new benchmark that evaluates whether review agents detect human-verified bugs. We build a data pipeline that mines bugs from repository history, and pair each bug with its introducing PR to form a review task. CRBugBench consists of 1,000 review tasks across 98 open-source GitHub repositories, and 800 additional tasks from proprietary industry repositories at one large company. We then use the benchmark to study agent review quality: We found that SOTA review agents do well: the best (claude-opus-5 with copilot-cli) catches 71.9% of bugs within its top-5 comments, up from 47.7% (opus-4.6) in five months of model evolution. Yet agents remain less effective on complex changes and bugs requiring non-local reasoning, with sharp performance drops on proprietary industry repositories where project context is more often needed. To improve bug detection, we found scaling compute helps most through prompt changes: exploration-nudging prompts gain up to 5.8% performance at 10% higher cost. From these findings, we derive concrete guidance for developers to adopt review agents - scaling review effort to PR complexity, steering agents by project priorities, supplying repository knowledge, dispatching models by PR characteristics, and monitoring quality as models evolve.</p></details> |  |
| **[A Multi-Agent LLM System for Automated Tuning of Specialized Neural Network Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13663v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Many machine learning approaches are two-level: an expert selects a class of methods, within this class the choice of key components is critical and usually manual. This paper addresses the automatic tuning of these components and proposes a framework transferable across models of different nature. The multi-agent large language model (LLM) framework has three roles---analyst, coder, and recorder~---with an experiment log and a sandboxed execution environment. Unlike systems that optimize the entire ML script or search architectures in a discrete space, the framework operates over an open but semantically narrow space: the agent generates code only for the specified components, leaving the overall network structure unchanged. The approach is validated in two directions: tuning a classification spiking neural network with correlation learning, and solving high-order differential equations with Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINN). Across five tasks---spiking classification on MNIST and Digits, and boundary-value problems for the generalized nonlinear Schrödinger equation (GNLSE) and Kuramoto--Sivashinsky (KS) equations---the agent outperforms the baselines: classification accuracy is 13 percentage points (MNIST) and 6 percentage points (Digits) higher; the error against the analytical single-soliton solution is reduced approximately 2491-fold; the invariant-preservation error drops approximately 325-fold in the selected GNLSE run with a Gaussian perturbation and by more than 100-fold on average over repeated initializations; and the mass drift drops 14-fold for the KS problem with a Gaussian perturbation. The framework thus tunes key components on tasks of different nature without changing its internal structure.</p></details> |  |
| **[A-SAW: The Agentic Software Architecture Workflow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13647v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI assistance in software engineering focuses on implementation and verification, leaving the design phase, and in particular the maintenance of architecture documentation, comparatively underserved. Architecture as Code makes architectural artifacts versionable, diffable, and machine-readable, but it does not reduce the effort of keeping them updated, so design documentation still ends up as a fragmented, author-dependent, and only partially trusted record of why a system looks the way it does. In this paper we introduce the Agentic Software Architecture Workflow (A-SAW), a process-level artifact that extends Architecture as Code with a persistent agent layer: an IDE-integrated agent drafts architecture documents from a version-controlled C4 model, a typed element catalog, and instrumented templates. Every draft passes a mandatory human review gate before it is accepted. We evaluate it in a large industrial organization along two tracks: a rubric assessment of 45 architecture documents including 13 matched source-regeneration pairs, and 13 task-anchored practitioner interviews. Our results show that the workflow collapses the between-author variance that made quality unpredictable. Intrinsic quality also rises, by a median of +19 points across the matched pair. The cohort has a mean of 58% and 89% bundle templates, catalog, agent, and a richer regeneration context. So they bound the agent's contribution from above rather than isolating it. In the agent-generated documents, completeness (100%), consistency (93%), and machine-readability (99%) reach or approach the rubric maximum, while traceability reaches 79%. Eleven of the 13 participants rejected agent output that no human had reviewed, and one warned that polished output may lead reviewers to skip the review. Our work supports keeping human review mandatory and concentrating it where the agent must infer beyond the repository.</p></details> |  |

| **[Bistable by Construction: Wall-Clock-Calibrated State Monitors Have No Moment-Detection Regime at Agent Cadence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.19386v2)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Runtime monitors for autonomous agents commonly threshold an accumulated internal state - a behavioural baseline, a drift statistic, or, in our prior work, a modelled affective state. | **[EmbodiedCLBench: Evaluating Continual Learning for Self-Evolving Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13021v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Continual learning requires models to accumulate past experience and transfer it to new tasks. With compositional generalization as the core evaluation principle, our benchmark evaluates whether agents can first learn from basic tasks and then recombine the acquired skills to solve complex, unseen advanced tasks. Based on EmbodiedCLBench, we conduct extensive experiments across multiple agent harnesses and learning methods, revealing consistent limitations and distinctive behaviors. Our results show that while advanced tasks can be hardly solved under zero-shot setting, the experience from basic tasks enables consistent improvement. Such benefit from the experience remains stable even if the task complexity increases. However, this improvement does not scale accordingly with the number of learning samples, and current agents cannot extract meaningful information from additional experience. Collectively, our findings highlight continual learning as a fundamental yet underexplored capability, and our benchmark offers valuable resources for designing effective self-evolving agents in embodied environments. In this work, we explain the connection between skill learning and empowerment. | **[Hindsight Structured Advice Distillation: Steering Executor Agents with Advisors Trained on Their Failures and Task Characteristics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13000v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Language-model agents are used for specialized work such as financial research, healthcare and law. ## Drug Design
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[A Large Scale Investigation of Scaling Limits in Chemical Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13408v3)** | 2026-10-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Chemical Language Models (CLMs) are increasingly used in de novo drug design, driven by recent growth in model scale, compute, and dataset size. However, the relationship between design choices, training dynamics, and downstream generation quality remains poorly understood. We present a compute-controlled scaling study of CLMs comprising more than 30,000 experiments across molecular representations (SMILES, SELFIES, SAFE), tokenizations (atom-level and byte-pair encoding), model scales (0.5M-1B parameters), leakage-controlled datasets (MOSES, ChEMBL, PubChem, ZINC-22), and architectures (decoder-only and encoder-decoder). By fitting IsoFLOP profiles, we establish clear scaling trends in pretraining loss, but find that these improvements do not translate into comparable gains in goal-directed molecular design. Layer-wise probing and sparse autoencoder analysis reveal continued development of chemical representations: chemical syntax saturates early, while semantic properties emerge more slowly and become increasingly accessible with further training and model scale. These representational gains coexist with diminishing improvements in goal-directed generation under the evaluated protocols and oracle budgets. Our resulting suite of models, NovoMolGen, achieves state-of-the-art results, outperforming prior CLMs and specialized generative models in goal-directed molecular generation across drug discovery tasks. These findings expose a disconnect between chemical representation learning and downstream molecular design, motivating the development of pretraining paradigms that more directly learn chemical semantics.</p></details> |  |
| **[One Sequence, Many Decodings: CAGenMol-2 Recasts Drug Design as Masked Molecular Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34301v1)** | 2026-09-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Drug design couples property evaluation, conditional generation, structure-based design, and local optimization, yet machine learning systems typically address these capabilities with separate task-specific models. We introduce CAGenMol-2, a masked diffusion molecular language model that represents molecules, continuous scalar properties, and 3D protein pockets within a single wrapped sequence. Within this pretrained interface, downstream operations are selected by which sequence regions are observed or masked at inference, allowing one checkpoint to perform property prediction, property- and pocket-conditioned generation, and partial-constraint design without task-specific architectures or backbone fine-tuning. We further propose Adaptive Fragment Optimization (AdaFO), a gradient-free mask-and-refill search that turns the masked decoder into an iterative local molecular optimizer. On CrossDocked2020, AdaFO increases Success Rate from 30.2\% to 70.8\%, the best reported under this protocol, while largely preserving drug-likeness and diversity. Finally, scaffold-preserving directional editing and CRBN/VHL case studies demonstrate its use in compound design workflows spanning local molecular editing, structure-based prioritization, and downstream simulation-based screening.</p></details> |  |
| **[PocketVE: Stable and Property-Guided Structure-Based Drug Design with Variance-Exploding Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08101v3)** | 2026-09-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Protein-conditioned 3D molecule generation is a central challenge in structure-based drug design, requiring a balance between pocket compatibility, molecular properties, and physical geometry. Specifically, PocketVE combines an EDM-style training and sampling setup for 3D denoising, classifier-free guidance for multi-property steering, and adaptive protein perturbation as a training-time pocket regularizer.On the CrossDocked2020 benchmark under GenBench3D, PocketVE improves Valid$_{3\text{D}}$ from 58.6 to 80.6 and reduces strain energy from 457.4 to 127.9 relative to its guided TAGMol architectural parent; relative to TargetDiff, it attains comparable Valid$_{3\text{D}}$ with lower strain energy (127.9 vs.\ 306.0), while retaining competitive docking and molecular-property scores under moderate guidance. Pocket-permutation and PoseCheck diagnostics further support pocket-specific spatial compatibility with reduced steric conflicts. Overall, the results suggest that geometric stability and inference-time property guidance should be considered as coupled design objectives.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by Neurips 2026</p></details> |
| **[A Systematic Evaluation of Molecule Generation Models for De Novo Drug Design: From Benchmarks to Practical Insights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10099v1)** | 2026-09-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Molecule generation has emerged as a powerful computational tool for de novo drug design, enabling the exploration of chemical space beyond the limits of conventional virtual screening. The field has progressed rapidly, driven by advances in molecular representations, generative architectures, and target-aware modeling strategies. However, existing reviews typically address specific model families or application scenarios in isolation, rather than offering an integrated perspective on how these components collectively form a coherent generation workflow. In this review, we present a comprehensive evaluation of molecule generation models for de novo drug design, covering 82 methods across five deep generative frameworks, including recurrent neural network (RNN)- and Transformer-based models, variational autoencoders (VAEs), generative adversarial networks (GANs), flow-based models, and diffusion models. We first summarize widely used benchmarks and molecular representations, and then examine the methodological principles underlying both general and pocket-conditioned generation. A central contribution of this work is a systematic synthesis and comparative analysis of reported performance across commonly used benchmarks and evaluation metrics. We also summarize representative experimentally validated case studies. Looking ahead, we discuss future directions in standardized 3D data, interaction-aware generation, receptor flexibility, and multi-objective molecular design, with the aim of improving the reliability and experimental relevance of molecule generation. All collected benchmark resources, evaluation metrics, and model references are provided in a publicly accessible repository at https://github.com/JacklinGroup/molecule-generation-review.</p></details> | <details><summary>This ...</summary><p>This document is the unedited Author's version of a Submitted Manuscript subsequently accepted for publication in [Journal of Chemical Information and Modeling]. To access the final published article</p></details> |
| **[FuseDiff: Symmetry-Preserving Joint Diffusion for Dual-Target Structure-Based Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.05567v2)** | 2026-09-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Dual-target structure-based drug design aims to generate a single ligand together with two pocket-specific binding poses, each compatible with a corresponding target pocket, enabling polypharmacological therapies with improved efficacy and reduced resistance.
